functions, their concavifications, and the inverse hazard
rates in Figure
4
. Observe that in this example, the solid dots
(the hazard rates at the consumer-optimal segmentation) are shifted away
from the white dots (the hazard rate in the aggregate market).

Figure 4
:
u_{k}
and
\overline{u}_{k}
for Example
2
.

4.4
Properties of the Consumer-Optimal Segmentation

Although we explicitly construct a market segmentation that attains the
upper bound of (
29
), the consumer-optimal segmentation that we
obtain by construction is not easy to express in closed form. However, we
can find a sharp description of the qualities that buyers will consume in
this segmentation. Furthermore, the properties we provide next hold across
all consumer-optimal segmentations, not just the one we obtain from
following the procedure described in the proof of Theorem
1
.

We first characterize the hazard rates of the demands in the support of a
consumer-optimal segmentation in terms of a solution to (
29
). We
denote by
\supp(\overline{u}_{k}(h))
the support of the concavification of

u
at
h
:

\supp(\overline{u}_{k}(h))\triangleq\{h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:u_{k}(h^{\prime})=\overline{u}_{k}(h^{\prime})\text{ and }\overline{u}_{k}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}h+(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}})h^{\prime})\text{ is linear in }{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}\in[0,1]\}.

In other words, the support consists of the hazard rates
h^{\prime}
where

u_{k}
and
\overline{u}_{k}
coincide, and
\overline{u}_{k}
is linear on
the interval between
h
and
h^{\prime}
.

Proposition 2 (Properties of Consumption)

A segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283
solves (
6
) if
and only if there exists some
D
solving (
29
) such that for every
x\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})
and
v_{k}\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}(x)
,

h_{k}^{x}\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}\left(\overline{u}_{k}\left(h_{k+1}^{D}\right)\right),

(34)

and, for all
k
,

\sum_{\{x\mid x_{k}>0\}}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)h_{k}^{x}=h_{k}^{D}.

(35)

This proposition provides general properties of the hazard rates of the
demand at any given value
v_{k}
in a consumer-optimal segmentation in
terms of the solution to (
29
). The expected hazard rate is
h_{k}^{D}
(see (
35
)) and the hazard rates are in the support of
\overline{u}
(see (
34
)). We recall that in any market buyer
v_{k}
will consume quality:

q=Q(v_{k}-h_{k}^{x}).

So, we can now translate these properties about hazard rates to properties
about the qualities consumed by buyers in a consumer-optimal segmentation.

Let
q^{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}_{k}
denote the qualities consumed by value
v_{k}
in some
market of segmentation
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}:

q^{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}_{k}\triangleq\big\{q\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mid q^{x}_{k}=q\text{ for
some }x\in\mathrm{supp}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})\big\}.

Proposition 3 (Monotonicity of Quality)

Let
\textstyle\mathchar 283
be a consumer-optimal segmentation. Then,
for all
k
,

\max\{q:q\in q^{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}_{k}\}\leq\min\{q:q\in q^{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}_{k+1}\}.

That is, the quality consumption of different values is totally ordered
between segments.

This proposition follows from the proof of Theorem
1
and the
result that every
h_{k}^{x}
must be in the support of
\overline{u}_{k}(h_{k}^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}})
. That consumption is monotone within segments is
immediate from incentive compatibility. However, the fact that monotonicity
also holds across segments is a special property of the consumer-optimal
segmentation.

We can then provide a limit on how much dispersion there is in the
consumption across values for any given value. We denote by

d^{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}_{k}\triangleq\max\{q:q\in q^{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}_{k}\}-\min\{q:q\in q^{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}_{k}\},

the dispersion of consumption in segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283
for value
v_{k}
. This
is the difference between the maximum and minimum quality purchased by value

v_{k}
across all markets in segmentation
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}.
We denote
\overline{Q}

the maximum efficient quality supplied to any value:

\overline{Q}\triangleq\argmax_{q\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\big\{v_{K}q-c(q)\big\}<\infty.

Since the seller will never supply an inefficiently high quality, the
quality supplied to every value will be below
\overline{Q}
. We now use
these definitions to bound the consumption dispersion.

Corollary 1 (Quality Dispersion)

For any consumer-optimal segmentation and
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}>0
,
there exists at most
\overline{Q}/{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}
different values
v_{k}
such that

d^{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}_{k}>{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}.

Hence, for any fixed level of dispersion
\textstyle\mathchar 271
, there is a bound on the
number of values that has a dispersion larger than
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}.
Importantly,
the bound does not depend on the aggregate market. An immediate implication
is that, if we approximate a absolutely continuous distribution with a limit
of increasingly finer discrete distributions, then in the limit almost every
type will consume a unique quality. In the following section, we provide
conditions such that the spirit of the result is satisfied exactly, that is,
each buyer consumes only one quality across all markets in a
consumer-optimal segmentation.

Finally, Proposition
2
allows us to obtain a upper bound
on the prices of any quality level in any consumer-optimal segmentation. For
this, first define:

\overline{h}_{k}=\argmax_{h}\big[u_{k}(h)\big]

(36)

Following (
34
) we have that value
v_{k}
always buys a quality
weakly larger than:

\underline{q}_{k}\triangleq Q(v_{k}-h_{k}).

We formalize this in the following corollary.

Corollary 2 (Minimum Quality)

In any consumer-optimal segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283
, any buyer
of type
v_{k}
present in market
x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})
consumes at least
quality
\underline{q}_{k}
. Furthermore, the bound is tight: there exists
an aggregate market under which no segmentation is optimal and every value
v_{k}
consumes
\underline{q}_{k}
.

We can thus bound the size of the inefficiencies in any consumer-optimal
segmentation. The surprising aspect of the result is that the bound can be
derived using only the value of a buyer and the cost function.

5
Computing the Value of Segmentation

The results in the previous section provided a characterization of the value
of the consumer-optimal segmentation and provided properties of the
qualities consumed by different buyers in the optimal segmentation. However,
in this characterization, the value of the consumer-optimal segmentation is
expressed as a solution to a maximization problem. This makes it difficult
to gauge when the gains can be large or small, or even when no segmentation
is indeed the consumer-optimal segmentation. We thus now provide conditions
under which the value of segmentation can be characterized more sharply.
First, we provide sufficient conditions for
D^{*}
to be the solution of (
29
). These conditions simplify the calculation of the value of the
consumer-optimal segmentation. However, even when
D^{*}
solves (
29
), segmentation may improve the consumer surplus through within-value
persuasion (for example, as in Section
3
). Hence, we also
characterize when no segmentation is optimal.

We begin by providing conditions in terms of the local value of segmentation

u
and the hazard rate in the aggregate market
h_{k}^{\ast}.
These
conditions will be easy-to-verify and help characterize the value of
segmentation. We then provide conditions on the cost function and the
distribution of values, which will be less general but easier to interpret.

5.1
When No Segmentation is Optimal

First, we provide a condition which guarantees
D^{\ast}
is a solution to (
29
).

Lemma 5 (No Between-Value Segmentation)

The solution to (
29
) is
D^{\ast}
if and only
if
\overline{u}_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{\ast})
is nondecreasing. In
particular, in this case, the consumer-optimal segmentation generates:

\max_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\in\Delta(\Delta V)}\sum_{x\in\mathrm{supp}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)U(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{\ast}_{k}\overline{u}_{k}\left({h_{k}^{\ast}}\right).

(37)

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
) Consider any segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283
and recall that
D^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}\prec D^{\ast}
. The difference between the RHS of (
29
)
at
D^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}
and at
D^{\ast}
can be bounded as

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{\ast}_{k}(\overline{u}_{k}(h_{k}^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}})-\overline{u}_{k}(h_{k}^{\ast}))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{\ast}_{k}(\overline{u}_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{\ast})(h_{k}^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}-h_{k}^{\ast}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\overline{u}_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{\ast})(v_{k+1}-v_{k})(D^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}_{k+1}-D^{\ast}_{k+1})

where the inequality uses the fact that
\overline{u}_{k}
is concave.
Following Theorem 2 of
Fan and
Lorentz 1954
we have that:

\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\overline{u}_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{\ast})(v_{k+1}-v_{k})(D^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}_{k+1}-D^{\ast}_{k+1})\leq 0,

which proves the result. To be precise in the use of Theorem 2 of
Fan and
Lorentz 1954
, we express our terms in their notation. We have the
function

\Phi(z,k)=-c_{k}z_{k}

which is linear and submodular in
(z_{k},k)
. The constants
c_{k}=\overline{u}_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{\ast})
are nondecreasing and
D^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}\prec D^{\ast}
, and hence

\sum_{j=1}^{K-1}\Phi((v_{j+1}-v_{j})D^{\ast}_{j+1},j)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{K-1}\Phi((v_{j+1}-v_{j})D^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}_{j+1},j).

Multiplying this inequality by
-1
yields

\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\overline{u}_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{\ast})(v_{k+1}-v_{k})(D^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}_{k+1}-D^{\ast}_{k+1})\leq 0

proving the lemma.

(
\Rightarrow
) In the proof of Theorem
1
, we show that for any
solution
D
of (
29
),
u^{\prime}(h^{D}_{k})
is nondecreasing; see
discussion surrounding (
A.9
).

The proof basically uses the concavity of
\overline{u}
and a classic
inequality for majorization constraints. The use of inequalities that rely
on majorizations has been used recently by
Kleiner
et al. 2021
. Unlike these
papers, we do not have a monotonicity constraint (
D^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}
might be
nondecreasing), but we can still use Theorem 2 in
Fan and
Lorentz 1954
. Hence, the
essence of the result is the same.

We now characterize when no segmentation is optimal. For this, we define:

O=\Big\{x\in\Delta V:u_{k}(h_{k}^{x})=\overline{u}_{k}(h_{k}^{x})\text{ for
all $k$, and }u_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{x})\text{ is nondecreasing in }k\Big\}.

This exactly characterizes the set of markets where no segmentation is
optimal.

Proposition 4 (No Segmentation)

The consumer-optimal segmentation is no segmentation if
and only if
x^{*}\in O
.

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
). Suppose
x^{*}\in O
. Then, following Lemma
5
, we have that
D^{*}
solves (
29
). Furthermore, since
u_{k}(h^{\ast}_{k})=\overline{u}_{k}(h^{\ast}_{k})
,

\max_{D\prec D^{*}}\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{*}_{k}\overline{u}_{k}(h^{D}_{k})=\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{*}_{k}\overline{u}_{k}(h^{\ast}_{k})=\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{*}_{k}u_{k}(h^{\ast}_{k}).

The second equality follows from the definition of
O
. Thus, no
segmentation is optimal.

(
\Rightarrow
). If the first condition is not satisfied, then:

\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{*}_{k}u_{k}(h^{\ast}_{k})<\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{*}_{k}\overline{u}_{k}(h^{\ast}_{k})\leq\max_{D\prec D^{*}}\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{*}_{k}\overline{u}_{k}(h^{D}_{k}),

contradicting that no segmentation is optimal. If the second condition is
not satisfied, then by Lemma
5
,

\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{*}_{k}u_{k}(h^{\ast}_{k})\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{*}_{k}\overline{u}_{k}(h^{\ast}_{k})<\max_{D\prec D^{*}}\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{*}_{k}\overline{u}_{k}(h^{D}_{k}),

which again contradicts that no segmentation is optimal.

We illustrate the set
O
in Figure
5
for when
c(q)=q^{2}/2

and
V=\{3,4,5\}
. Since there are three values, the simplex is a subset of
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
. The three vertices of the triangles represent the three
markets in which only one of the values is present. Then, any point in the
triangle represents a market where the composition of values is represented
by the distance to the vertices. In this graph, we also illustrate the set
of regular distributions that have positive virtual values (that is,
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 286$}}}_{k}^{x}
is increasing in
k
and non-negative for all
k\in\{1,...,K\}
).
For reference, these are the distributions under which there is no exclusion
of any value and there is full separation. We can see there is a large class
of markets under which no segmentation is optimal. This is despite the fact
that in all these markets the allocation is inefficient. However, we find
that in some markets, segmentation can improve consumer surplus even when
the distribution is regular and there is no exclusion of any values.

Figure 5
:
Illustration of
O
and regular markets with quadratic costs.

5.2
Conditions on Cost Function and Aggregate Market

We now provide sufficient conditions for
\overline{u}_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{\ast})
to be nondecreasing in terms of the primitives, the cost
function and the distribution of values in the aggregate market. Assume that

c(q)
is thrice differentiable, and that:

\inf_{q}\ \frac{c^{\prime\prime\prime}(q)q}{c^{\prime\prime}(q)}\geq-1.

(38)

This condition holds whenever
c^{\prime}(q)
is convex or not too concave.
This assumption allows us to characterize the shape of
\overline{u}_{k}
.

Lemma 6 (Concave
u_{k}
)

Under (
38
), the concavification of
u_{k}

is given by:

\overline{u}_{k}(h)=\begin{cases}u_{k}(h)&\text{if }h\leq\overline{h}_{k};\\
u_{k}(\overline{h}_{k})&\text{if }h>\overline{h}_{k}.\end{cases}

where
\overline{h}_{k}
is defined in (
36
). Furthermore,
\overline{u}_{k}(h)
is strictly concave for all
h<\overline{h}_{k}.

In other words, the concavification is defined by a cutoff
\overline{h}_{k}
, where the concavification is constant for hazard rates above the cutoff
and is equal to
u_{k}
below the cutoff. An immediate corollary of this is
that in the consumer-optimal segmentation, the quality consumed is unique.

Corollary 3 (Unique Consumption)

Under (
38
), in any consumer-optimal segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283
,
q_{k}^{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}
is a singleton for every
k
.

Hence, we obtain that each buyer consumes only one quality. This refines
Corollary
1
, which states that with many different buyer
values, almost all of them consumed essentially the same quality.

For the aggregate market, we assume that:

(v_{k+1}-v_{k})\frac{D_{k}^{\ast}}{x_{k}^{\ast}}\text{ is decreasing in }k.

(39)

If
(v_{k+1}-v_{k})
is constant, i.e. the values are a uniform grid, we
recover the well-known monotone hazard rate (MHR) condition. For continuous
random variables, this is also equivalent to requiring the distribution to
be log-concave (see
Bagnoli and
Bergstrom 2005
for common distributions that satisfy this
condition and other properties of these distributions).

Lemma 7 (No Between-Value Segmentation with Convex Marginal Cost)

If (
38
)-(
39
) are satisfied, then
\overline{u}_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{\ast})
is nondecreasing and hence the
consumer surplus generated by the consumer-optimal segmentation is (
37
).

Following Lemma
5
-
7
, we thus have that under (
38
)-(
39
), the solution to (
29
) is
D^{\ast}
. Note that the aggregate market in Example
2
did not satisfy
the monotone hazard rate condition, which explains why
D^{\ast}
did not
solve (
29
).

We can now provide a sharp characterization of the consumer-optimal
segmentation.

Proposition 5 (Gains From Segmentation)

Under (
38
)-(
39
), the gains from
the consumer-optimal segmentation are:

\max_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}\left[\sum_{x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)U(x)\right]-U(x^{\ast})=\sum_{k=1}^{\widehat{k}-1}x^{\ast}_{k}(u_{k}(\overline{h}_{k})-u_{k}({h}_{k}^{\ast})),

where
\widehat{k}=\min\Big\{k\mid h_{k}^{\ast}\leq\overline{h}_{k}\Big\}.

The proposition states that the consumer-optimal segmentation is given by
the expected difference between
u
and
\overline{u}
. Furthermore, this
difference will be non-zero for values below a cutoff. Hence, the only gains
for consumers come from changes in the consumption of relatively low values.

The proof of Proposition
5
explicitly constructs one
consumer-optimal segmentation, which is helpful for understanding how the
segmentation is affecting consumption. In this segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283
, each
market
x\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})
has support
\{v_{j},v_{j+1},\dots,v_{K}\}
for some
j\leq\widehat{k}
. Within every market
x
, for every
k>\widehat{k}
,

h_{k}^{x}=h_{k}^{\ast},

meaning high-value buyers consume the same quality as in the aggregate
market. On the other hand, for all
k\leq\widehat{k}
,

h_{k}^{x}=\overline{h}_{k}\leq h_{k}^{\ast},

so low-value buyers consume a higher quality than they would in the absence
of segmentation. We know there is no within-value persuasion, so all buyers
of a given type consume the same quality across segments. Whether the
consumer-optimal segmentation raises the consumption of type
v_{k}
depends
on how
h_{k}^{\ast}
compares to
\overline{h}_{k}
.

Proposition
5
also allows us to identify conditions so that
the cutoff is trivial, so there are no benefits from segmentation at all.

Lemma 8 (No Segmentation—Convex MC)

Under (
38
)-(
39
), if
h_{1}^{\ast}\leq\overline{h}_{1}
, then no segmentation is the consumer-optimal segmentation.

This result is in contrast to
Haghpanah and
Siegel 2023
, where with finite goods,
segmentation is generically (in the space of possible markets) beneficial to
consumers. Here, with a continuum of qualities, we obtain a large class of
aggregate markets in which no segmentation is optimal.

6
Isoelastic Cost

A particular functional form satisfying (
38
) which gives us
interesting comparative statics is the isoelastic cost case:

c(q)=\frac{q^{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}},\ {{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}>1.

(40)

The parameter
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}=c^{\prime}(q)q/c(q)
indicates the cost elasticity.
This parametric form for the cost function will allow us to simplify
expressions and provide expression that permit easy comparative statics with
respect to
\textstyle\mathchar 269
. We continue to assume (
39
), the monotone
hazard rate condition on the aggregate market.
4
4

4

With isoelastic costs, this assumption can be mildly relaxed to the weaker
condition that, for all
k
,

\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 286$}}}_{k+1}^{\ast}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 286$}}}_{k}^{\ast}}{v_{k+1}-v_{k}}\geq\frac{1}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}}.

We can describe the consumer-optimal segmentation using a discrete version
of the familiar notion of demand elasticity
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}}_{k}^{x}
:

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}}_{k}^{x}\triangleq\frac{x_{k}}{D_{k+1}}\cdot\frac{v_{k}}{v_{k+1}-v_{k}}=\frac{v_{k}}{h_{k}^{x}}.

That is, in market
x
at price
v_{x}
if the price of a good increases by
a fraction
(v_{k+1}-v_{k})/v_{k}
of the original price, then demand will
decrease by a fraction
x_{k}/D_{k+1}
, which is
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}}_{k}^{x}
. Note that
for measuring the price increase we use as base the pre-increase price while
to measure the demand decrease we use the post-increase demand. When working
with continuous demands this is obviously irrelevant, while for discrete
demands there are many natural ways to extend the definition. We take the
definition as it is more convenient for the notation and algebra, but
obviously the choice becomes irrelevant as the grid of possible values
become fine enough. We now describe the demand elasticity in the segments of
a consumer-optimal segmentation.

Proposition 6 (Optimal Segmentation—Isoelastic Cost)

Under (
39
)-(
40
), in any
consumer-optimal segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283
, for every
x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})
and
every
k
such that
x_{k}>0
:

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}}_{k}^{x}=\begin{cases}\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}-1}&\text{if }k<\widehat{k};\\
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}}_{k}^{\ast}&\text{if }k\geq\widehat{k}.\end{cases}

where
\widehat{k}=\min\left\{k\mid h_{k}^{\ast}\leq\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}-1}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}}\right\}

Proof of Proposition
6
.

The isoelastic cost function satisfies (
38
). Following Lemma
7
, we know that
\overline{u}_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{\ast})
is nondecreasing and following Lemma
5
we know that this implies that the solution to (
29
) is
D^{\ast}
. Finally, since
\overline{u}_{k}^{\prime}(h_{k}^{\ast})
is nondecreasing, we know that
h^{*}_{k}\leq\overline{h}_{k}
if and only if
k\leq\widehat{k}
, where
\widehat{k}
is defined in Proposition
5
and
\overline{h}_{k}
is defined in (
36
).

Following Lemma
6
we know that
u_{k}(h)=\overline{u}_{k}(h)
for all
h\leq\overline{h}_{k}.
Following Proposition
2
, we have that in any consumer-optimal segmentation the hazard rates satisfy:

h_{k}^{x}=\begin{cases}\overline{h}_{k}&\text{if }k<\widehat{k};\\
h_{k}^{\ast}&\text{if }k\geq\widehat{k}.\end{cases}

Finally, we have that:

\overline{h}_{k}=\frac{v_{k}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}-1)}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}}.

Re-arranging terms, we get the result.

We thus have that in the consumer-optimal segmentations the demand
elasticity in every segment takes two forms. Either the demand has the same
elasticity as the aggregate market, or it has a constant elasticity
determined by the cost function. Note that we might still have that the
support of values differs across markets, so the price of goods might change
across markets in the consumer-optimal segmentation. As
\textstyle\mathchar 269
increases,
the demand elasticity falls, and the range of consumer values consuming more
than they would under no segmentation shrinks.

The demand elasticity in the consumer-optimal segmentation is weakly
decreasing in the cost elasticity. The intuition is that with a more elastic
cost, the demand needs to be more inelastic for the seller to provide a
relative higher quality. Hence, a more inelastic demand is necessary to
reduce the inefficiencies.

We can also recover the unit demand case of
Bergemann
et al. 2015
by taking the
limit as
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}\rightarrow\infty
. Then, we get that in every segment,
demand is unit-elastic, and the set of values who consume more than under no
segmentation is

\Big\{v_{k}\mid{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}}_{k}^{\ast}\leq 1\Big\}

which, with the MHR assumption, is the set of values excluded in the
aggregate market. Additionally, observe that

Q(v_{k}-\overline{h}_{k})=\left(\frac{v_{k}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}-1}}\xrightarrow{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}\rightarrow\infty}1.

Thus, in the consumer-optimal segmentation all consumers are allocated the
good, and the segmentation is socially efficient.

Finally, we describe how the potential gains from segmentation changes with
\textstyle\mathchar 269
. For this, we denote by
O_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}
the set of markets under which no
segmentation is optimal when the cost elasticity is
\textstyle\mathchar 269
.

Proposition 7 (Comparative Statics)

For any
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}^{\prime}<{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}
,
O_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}^{\prime}}\subset O_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}
.

Hence, as the cost becomes more elastic (
\textstyle\mathchar 269
decreases), the potential
gains from segmentation increases. This is despite the fact that even in
some inefficient markets, no segmentation can be optimal. In contrast, in
the limit
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}\rightarrow\infty
, no segmentation is optimal only if
the allocation in the aggregate market is efficient. One could have thought
that this means that it is relatively unlikely to find a market in which no
segmentation is optimal. However, the conclusion is the opposite: in the
limit
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}\rightarrow\infty
the cost is very inelastic which reduces
the potential benefits from segmentation.

7
Surplus-Sharing Segmentations

So far, this paper has focused on the consumer-optimal segmentation, the
segmentation which maximizes consumer surplus. One might naturally wonder
whether our approach can be used to characterize the entire surplus-sharing
frontier, i.e. the set of all possible divisions of surplus under
some
segmentation. This would produce a generalization of the “surplus
triangle” of
Bergemann
et al. 2015
. Of course, the segmentation will also change
how the seller prices the different goods, and so it will also change the
inefficiencies.

It turns out that by appropriately modifying the objective function, our
same approach can be used to produce the entire Pareto frontier,
representing all Pareto-efficient outcomes. For the remainder of the surplus
frontier, while the (modified) concavification problems remains a valid
outer bound, this bound may not be tight. However, under binary values
K=2
, or if we assume isoelastic costs and MHR, the bound once again becomes
tight and our concavification bound characterizes the entire set of
achievable outcomes.

7.1
Pareto-Efficient Segmentations

Consider the problem of finding, for some
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\in[0,1]
, a
segmentation which solves

\max_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\in\Delta(\Delta V)}\sum_{x\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)\Big[{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\Pi(x)+(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}})U(x)\Big].

It will be convenient to rewrite this as

\max_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\in\Delta(\Delta V)}\sum_{x\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)\Big[{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}W(x)+(1-2{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}})U(x)\Big],

where
W(x)
is the total welfare in a regular market
x
. By Lemma
4
, it continues to be without loss to restrict attention to regular
markets, so we can write
W
as

W(x)\triangleq\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{k}(h_{k}^{x})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}v_{k}Q(v_{k}-h_{k}^{x})-c(Q(v_{k}-h_{k}^{x})).

We then define the local objective function
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}},k}
in a
manner similar to the consumer surplus:

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}_{k,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}(h)\triangleq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}w_{k}(h)+(1-2{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}})u_{k}(h).

Then, the problem can be written as

\max_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\in\Delta(\Delta V)}\sum_{x\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x_{k}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}},k}(h_{k}^{x})\right]+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}x^{\ast}_{K}w_{K}(0),

where the last term comes from the fact that
h_{K}^{x}=0
always. Since
Section
4
uses no assumptions on the shape of
u_{k}
, it is
no surprise that the same result goes through by just replacing
u_{k}
with

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}},k}
.

Theorem 2 (Pareto-Efficient Segmentations)

Every Pareto-efficient segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283

satisfies, for some
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\in[0,1]
,

\sum_{x\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)\Big[{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\Pi(x)+(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}})U(x)\Big]=\max_{D\prec D^{\ast}}\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}x^{\ast}_{k}\overline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}}_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}},k}(h_{k}^{D})+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}x^{\ast}_{K}w_{K}(0).

(41)

The proof of this theorem completely mirrors that of Theorem
1
.
Note that for
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\geq\frac{1}{2}
, the solution will be first degree
(perfect) price discrimination, which maximizes social surplus and produces
zero consumer surplus.

7.2
Surplus-Sharing Frontier

In general, the entire frontier can be found by finding segmentations which
solve

\max_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\in\Delta(\Delta V)}\sum_{x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)\Big[e_{1}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\Pi(x)+e_{2}(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}})U(x)\Big],

for
e_{1},e_{2}\in\{-1,+1\}
and
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\in[0,1]
. As before, it will be
convenient to rewrite this as

\max_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\in\Delta(\Delta V)}\sum_{x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)\Big[e_{1}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\Pi(x)+\left(e_{2}(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}})-e_{1}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\right)U(x)\Big].

We thus define our local objective function

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}},e,k}(h)=e_{1}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}w_{k}(h)+\big[e_{2}(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}})-e_{1}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\big]u_{k}(h).

The first step of the proof of Theorem
1
, showing that the
concavification bound is an upper bound, still applies, giving us an “outer
bound” on the surplus frontier.

Lemma 9 (Outer Bound on Surplus-Sharing Frontier)

For any
e\in\{-1,+1\}^{2}
and
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\in[0,1]
,

\max_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\in\Delta(\Delta V)}\sum_{x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)\Big[e_{1}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\Pi(x)+e_{2}(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}})U(x)\Big]\leq\max_{D\prec D^{\ast}}\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\overline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}}_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}},e,k}(h_{k}^{D})+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}},e,K}(0).

(42)

Theorem
2
tells us that for
e=(+1,+1)
, the boundary
of the surplus frontier and the outcome corresponding to the solution of (
42
) coincide. However, in general, this is not true.
The reason is that in the proofs of Theorem
1
and
2
, we show the upper bound is implementable with
segmentations over regular markets. A crucial step of the proof is noticing
that the first-order condition governing the optimal
D
implies a
submodularity property (Lemma
5
) which guarantees
regularity. For general
(e,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}})
, we lose this submodularity, and hence
the regularity guarantee.

The concavification bound of (
42
) is tight whenever
K=2
, since all binary distributions are regular. We can also recover the
tightness of the bound by imposing the isoelastic functional form, along
with monotone hazard rate. In particular, suppose that the cost function is:

c(q)=\frac{q^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}},\ {{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}\geq 2,

(43)

and again impose the MHR condition (
39
). With these two
assumptions, the entire surplus boundary can be recovered from the
concavification bound (
42
). In fact, the solution is
attained by
D=D^{\ast}
, as it was in Proposition
6
.

Proposition 8 (Surplus-Sharing Frontier)

Under (
39
) and (
43
), the
surplus-sharing frontier attains the concavification bound of (
42
) with equality at
D=D^{\ast}
:

\max_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\in\Delta(\Delta V)}\sum_{x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)\Big[e_{1}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\Pi(x)+e_{2}(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}})U(x)\Big]=\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\overline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}}_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}},e,k}(h_{k}^{\ast})+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}},e,K}(0)

for all
e\in\{-1,+1\}^{2}
and
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}\in[0,1]
.

The functional form assumption (
43
) is only used to prove
tightness of the concavification bound when
e=(-1,+1)
, corresponding to
the “lower right” section of the boundary, which is the most difficult
case to deal with. The weaker requirement that
c^{\prime\prime\prime}(q)\geq 0
is sufficient for the other cases.

Figure
6
shows the surplus-sharing frontier for
quadratic costs,
c(q)=q^{2}/2
, when
V=\{1,2\}
and
x^{\ast}=(\frac{3}{5},\frac{2}{5})
. Note that the set of possible surplus divisions is a convex
subset of the “surplus triangle” of
Bergemann
et al. 2015
, which is the triangle
formed by the constraints that (1)
U\geq 0
, (2)
\Pi\geq\Pi^{\ast}
, the
profit in the aggregate market, and (3)
U+\Pi
does not exceed the total
available surplus in the market.

Figure 6
:
Surplus-sharing frontier with quadratic cost.

8
Concavification and Extreme Points

Our results arise as a result of concavification arguments; this stands in
contrast to the existing literature on price discrimination, which has taken
different routes to understanding how market segmentation can benefit
consumers. In this section, we discuss some of the main results in the
literature and contrast them to our results.

8.1
Extreme Points

Given a menu
p
, we can consider the (possibly empty) set of markets where
p
is optimal:

X_{p}\triangleq\left\{x\in\Delta V\mid\Pi(x,p)\geq\Pi(x,p^{\prime})\ \forall p^{\prime}\right\}.

(44)

The set
X_{p}
is compact and convex. An
extreme point
of
X_{p}
is
a point which cannot be represented by convex combination of other points in

X_{p}
. We will also refer to markets in the topological interior of
X_{p}
,
meaning they are neither extreme points nor on the boundary of
X_{p}
.
Consequently, we call such an
x
an
interior market
. By the
Minkowski-Caratheodory Theorem (
Simon 2011
, Theorem 8.11),
X_{p}
is equal
to the convex hull of its extreme points.

We denote by
p^{*}
the optimal price in the aggregate market, which we
assume in this section that is uniquely defined. We also assume that the
allocation in the aggregate market is inefficient to avoid analyzing trivial
cases. The set
X_{p^{*}}
identifies the set of all markets
x^{\prime}\in X_{p^{*}}
where the seller’s optimal price remains the same as in the
aggregate market. In particular, the extreme points of this set can be
useful to identify the consumer-optimal segmentation in some situations.

To illustrate this, analyze the case in which the seller has an indivisible
good for sale. We obtain this by assuming that:

c(q)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }q\leq 1;\\
\infty&\text{if }q>0.\end{cases}

(45)

This is the case studied by
Bergemann
et al. 2015
. In this case we have that the
extreme points are sufficient to understand the consumer-optimal
segmentation (and, in fact, they are sufficient to understand the welfare
implications of third-degree price discrimination).

Proposition 9 (Optimal Segmentation—Single Good)

If the cost function is (
45
), then there exists a consumer-optimal
segmentation that places weight only on the extreme points of
X_{p^{*}}.

Bergemann
et al. 2015
also show that using the extremal points of
X_{p^{\ast}}

allow implementing an efficient allocation without increasing the seller’s
profits (relative to the aggregate market), and hence it must be the
consumer-optimal segmentation. A remarkable aspect of this result is that
the extreme points also have a very simple structure.

With more than one good one can continue to follow this approach. In
particular consider a cost function
c(q)
which is piecewise linear with
kinks at integer values of
q
, i.e. when
q\in\{1,2,\dots,\}
. We can
interpret this as the cost function of a seller that has many (but discrete)
goods for sale, where a non-integer
q
is a randomization between the
neighboring two integer values.
5
5

5

That is, the buyer gets quality
\lceil q\rceil
with probability
(q-\lfloor q\rfloor)
and
\lfloor q\rfloor
with probability
(\lceil q\rceil-q)
, where
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
is the floor function and
\lceil\cdot\rceil
is the ceiling function.
We can then try to apply the
same logic as in
Bergemann
et al. 2015
, but we obtain a much weaker result.

Proposition 10 (Pareto Improvements)

If
x^{*}
is in the interior of
X_{p^{*}}
and the allocation is
inefficient in the aggregate market, then there is a segmentation that
places weight only on the extreme points of
X_{p^{*}}
that increases
consumer surplus and keeps profits constant.

An immediate corollary is that for generic aggregate markets, there is a
segmentation that Pareto improves the welfare generated by the aggregate
market. This was also proved in much greater generality by
Haghpanah and
Siegel 2023
.

There are two drawbacks with this approach. First, segmentations that place
weight only on the extreme points of
X_{p^{\ast}}
are not sufficient to
generate the consumer-optimal segmentation. Hence, it is necessary to study
segmentations that place weight on all extreme points of all sets of the
form
X_{p}
, and not just
X_{p^{\ast}}
. Second, the number of extreme
points grows quickly with the number of goods. To illustrate this, we
exemplify what happens as the number of goods grows to infinite.

Proposition 11 (Extreme Markets with a Continuum of Goods)

If
c^{\prime}
is strictly increasing for all
q
, then for every strictly
regular market
x
that induces no exclusion (that is,
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 286$}}}^{x}_{k}
is
strictly increasing in
k
and positive for all
k
),
X_{p^{x}}
is a
singleton. In particular,
x
is a extreme market of
X_{p^{x}}
.

We thus have that as the number of markets grow, essentially every relevant
market is an extreme market (recall from Lemma
4
we can focus on
regular markets). Since we do not know whether the consumer-optimal
segmentation will place weights only on the extreme points of
X_{p^{*}}
,
this approach does not allow narrowing the class of markets that need to be
considered when there are many goods. Furthermore, it is clear that in
general the gains from segmentations will become negligible as the number of
goods increase, unless we allow for non-local segmentations. Thus, while
Proposition
10
remains valid for any finite number of goods, its
usefulness becomes negligible as the number of goods grow.

Appendix
B
contains additional discussion and results for
the discrete good case. We also show how concavification can be used to
derive the extreme points in closed form.

8.2
Local Segmentations

We can use the set
O
to characterize the set of markets that are in the
support of a consumer-optimal segmentation. For the second part we first
observe that in any consumer-optimal segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283
, if
x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})
then no segmentation must be the consumer-optimal segmentation
when the aggregate market is
x
. After all, if there is an improvement for
consumers, these gains could have been also realized when the aggregate
market is
x^{*}
.

Corollary 4 (Support of Consumer-Optimal Segmentations)

Any consumer-optimal segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283
has support
\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})\subset O
.

While the set
O
restricts the set of possible markets, this can still be a
larger set. However, one can build a consumer-optimal segmentation in which
one restricts attention to the closest markets to
x^{*}.
To formalize this,
for any subset of markets
M\subset\Delta V
, we denote by
\mathrm{co}(M)

the convex hull of
M
. And, denote by
\overline{\mathrm{co}}(M)
the
convex hull of
M
without the points
M
themselves:

\overline{\mathrm{co}}(M)\triangleq{\mathrm{co}}(M)\backslash M.

We say
M
are local extreme points of
x^{*}
if
M\subset O
,
x\in\mathrm{co}(M)
and
\overline{co}(M)\cap O=\{\emptyset\}
. In other words, the
markets
M
are local extreme points of
x^{*}
if markets
M
belong to
O
,
they allow segmenting
x^{*}
and there is no market in
O
that can
nontrivially be segmented by markets in
M.

Proposition 12 (Constructing Consumer-Optimal Segmentations)

For all
x^{*}\not\in O
, there exists a
consumer-optimal segmentation that has support only on local extreme markets
of
x^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
\preceq
be the Blackwell order on
\Delta(\Delta V)
, that is, for any

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}^{\prime}\in\Delta(\Delta V)
,
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\preceq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}^{\prime}
if
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}^{\prime}
is a mean-preserving spread of
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}.
Let
S^{*}

be the set of all consumer-optimal segmentations. Let
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\in S^{*}
be
such that
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\preceq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}^{\prime}
for all
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}^{\prime}\in S^{\ast}
.
Following Proposition
4
, we know that
\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})\subset O
. Now, suppose
\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})
is not a local extreme market of
x^{*}
.
Then, there exists
\widehat{x}
such that
\widehat{x}\in\overline{\mathrm{co}}(\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})).
Let
\widehat{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}
be a non-trivial segmentation of
\widehat{x}
with support
\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}^{\prime})\subset\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}).
We
now consider the following segmentation:

\widetilde{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)=\begin{cases}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}\widehat{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)&\text{if }x\neq\widehat{x};\\
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(\widehat{x})+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}&\text{if }x=\widehat{x},\end{cases}

where
\textstyle\mathchar 271
is small enough such that
\widetilde{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)\geq 0
for
all
x
. The utility of segmentation
\widetilde{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}
can be written as
follows:

\sum_{x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}\widetilde{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)U(x)=\sum_{x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)U(x)+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}\left(U(\widehat{x})-\sum_{x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}\widehat{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)U(x)\right).

Following Proposition
4
we have that:

U(\widehat{x})-\sum_{x\in\supp({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})}\widehat{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}(x)U(x)\geq 0.

Hence,
\widetilde{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}
is also a consumer-optimal segmentation. However,
we also have that
\widetilde{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}\preceq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}
, so we reach a
contradiction.

The proposition identifies a set of candidates markets that can be part of a
consumer-optimal segmentation and then states that one can reduce the search
to the closest markets among the candidate ones (that is, closest to the
aggregate market). As a way to illustrate how much Proposition
12
narrows the search of a consumer-optimal segmentation, we
note that it is possible to restrict attention to consumer-optimal
segmentations that place positive weight on the boundary of
O
. The
boundary of
O
is highlighted in Figure
5
. In this sense,
it shares a similar spirit as the work of
Bergemann
et al. 2015
(Proposition
9
): we identify potential markets that can be in the support of an
optimal segmentation and then show we just need to search among the closest
ones.

9
Conclusion

This paper has characterized how market segmentation affects consumer
welfare when monopolists can engage in both second- and third-degree price
discrimination. Our analysis yields several key insights. First,
consumer-optimal segmentation maintains consistent quality provision across
segments while allowing price variation. Second, the benefits of
segmentation depend critically on demand elasticities and cost structures,
with no segmentation being optimal when aggregate demand is sufficiently
elastic.

These theoretical results have direct practical implications. For
competition authorities, they suggest that market segmentation should be
evaluated based on observable market characteristics rather than prohibited
categorically. The finding that quality provisions remain consistent across
segments provides a potential metric for identifying harmful segmentation
practices. For firms, our characterization of optimal segmentation
strategies offers guidance for designing market segmentation policies that
balance profit maximization with consumer welfare.

Our analysis also connects to broader debates about big data and
personalized pricing in digital markets. While enhanced ability to segment
markets could enable more sophisticated price discrimination, our results
suggest this may benefit consumers when properly structured. However, the
conditions we identify for beneficial segmentation—particularly regarding
demand elasticities and cost structures—may help guide regulatory policy.

Several important directions remain for future research. First, extending
the analysis to competitive markets as in the recent analysis of
Bergemann
et al. 2023
for single unit demand would provide insight into how market
structure affects optimal segmentation. Second, empirical work testing our
theoretical predictions about the relationship between demand elasticities
and optimal segmentation would be valuable.

Appendix A
Proof Details

In these proofs, we will at times refer to an indexed list of markets
x^{\ell}
. In these cases, a superscript
\ell
is shorthand for market
x^{\ell}
, e.g.
h^{1}
means
h^{x^{1}}
.

Proof of Lemma
4
.

We fix a market
x
and let
p
be a seller-optimal price menu. Let
X_{p}
be
the set of markets where
p
is optimal:

X_{p}\triangleq\left\{x^{\prime}\in\Delta V:p\in\arg\max\ \sum_{k}x^{\prime}_{k}\Pi(v_{k},p)\right\}.

It is clear that
X
is convex and compact. Following the Krein-Milman
theorem, we can write
x
as a linear combination of extreme points of
X
.
Furthermore, by Caratheodory’s theorem, we can write it as a linear
combination of at most
K
extreme points.

It thus suffices to show that every extreme point of
X_{p}
is regular.
Suppose that
y
is an extreme point of
X_{p}
and is not regular. Then there
exists
v_{k}
such that

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 295$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 295$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 295$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 295$}}}^{D}_{k+1}<{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 295$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 295$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 295$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 295$}}}^{D}_{k}.

Consider the binary segmentation supported on the following markets:

y^{+}_{i}=\begin{cases}y_{i}&\text{if }i\not\in\{k,k+1\};\\
y_{i}+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}&\text{if }i=k;\\
y_{i}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}&\text{if }i=k+1,\end{cases}\quad y^{-}_{i}=\begin{cases}y_{i}&\text{if }i\not\in\{k,k+1\}^{\prime}\\
y_{i}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}&\text{if }i=k;\\
y_{i}+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}&\text{if }i=k+1,\end{cases}

where
\textstyle\mathchar 271
is small enough such that
y_{+}(v_{k+1}),y_{-}(v_{k})\geq 0
.
It is easy to verify that
y_{+},y_{-}\in X
and that

y=\frac{y_{+}+y_{-}}{2}.

Thus,
y
is not an extreme point of
X_{p}
, a contradiction.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

In the main text, we showed that (
29
) is an upper bound. Next, we
show that there exists a segmentation which exactly achieves the average
hazard rate at every point, i.e., for every
x\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})
and

v_{k}\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}(x)
,
h_{k}^{x}=h_{k}^{D}
.

Lemma A.1 (Majorized
D
are Feasible)

For any
D
such that
D\prec D^{\ast}
, there exists a
segmentation
\textstyle\mathchar 283
such that for every
