{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}},k}^{\prime}(\hat{h}_{k})=(v_{k}-\hat{h}_{k})^{\frac{1}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}-1}-1}\left(v_{k}-\hat{h}_{k}-\frac{1+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}-1}\hat{h}_{k}\right).

Since
\hat{h}_{k}
is a linear function of
v_{k}
, and
v_{k}-\hat{h}_{k}\geq 0
in the relevant parameter region, this is increasing in
v_{k}
. This completes the proof.

Appendix B
Additional Results: Discrete Goods

B.1
Consumer-Optimal Segmentation

We model the discrete good environment by taking
c(q)
to be convex and
piecewise linear between integer values of
q
; equivalently, we take
c^{\prime}(q)
to be a step function. For notational ease, assume that
Q\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}\}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,Q\}
, denote
the different values of
c^{\prime}(q)
.

Such a
c(q)
is a piecewise linear approximation of some smooth function
\hat{c}(q)
. In particular, we can think of
u_{k}
as being equal to the
associated
\hat{u}_{k}
when
v_{k}-h\in\{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}\}
, and otherwise is
equal to the linear interpolation between these points. That makes
\overline{u}_{k}
itself a piecewise linear approximation of
\mathop{\mathrm{c}av}[\hat{u}_{k}]
.

This observation by itself is not particularly useful. However, combined
with an assumption that replicates the conditions of Section
5
,
we are able to recover many of the results from before. In particular, if:

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i+1}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}\text{ is increasing}

(B.1)

then
c
is a linear approximation of some
\hat{c}
satisfying (
38
). Thus, the concavification is equal to a piecewise linear
approximation of
\mathop{\mathrm{c}av}[\hat{u}_{k}]
up to the maximum, which is

i_{k}^{\ast}=\argmax_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\big[i\cdot(v_{k}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i})\big].

We can invert this condition by noting that

i_{k}^{\ast}=\max_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\Big\{i\mid i\cdot(v_{k}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i})\geq(i-1)\cdot(v_{k}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i-1})\Big\}=\max_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\Big\{i\mid v_{k}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}\geq(i-1)({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i-1})\Big\}.

This gives a discrete counterpart to Corollary
2
.

Corollary 5 (Minimum Quality with Convex Discrete Marginal Cost)

Under (
B.1
), every
v_{k}
such that

v_{k}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}\geq(i-1)({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i-1})

consumes quality at least
i
.

By imposing the MHR condition (
39
), we can also extend Proposition
5
.

Proposition B.1 (Optimal Segmentation with Convex Discrete Marginal Cost)

Under (
39
) and (
B.1
),
in any consumer-optimal segmentation, for every market
x\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})
and every consumer of type
v_{k}
,

q_{k}^{x}\in\begin{cases}\{i^{\ast}\}&\text{if }h_{k}^{\ast}\geq v_{k}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i^{\ast}};\\
\{q_{k}^{\ast},q_{k}^{\ast}+1\}&\text{if }h_{k}^{\ast}<v_{k}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i^{\ast}}.\end{cases}

Proposition
4
does not go through—this can be seen
immediately from Proposition
B.1
, as the quality
provided to the same type may differ by 1 across segments. As a result,
generically, some segmentation is always beneficial. This fact is also the
main result of
Haghpanah and
Siegel 2023
. However, if we consider a discrete
approximation of a continuous cost function that satisfies Proposition
4
, the benefits of segmentation are small: equal to the gain
from concavifying the hazard rate around the two nearby points of
v_{k}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}
. As the number of goods grows, i.e. as
c(q)
approaches
a smooth function, this gain goes to 0.

B.2
From Concavification to Extreme Points

In this section, we show that concavification can be used to explicitly
derive the extreme points in a discrete good setting. We will make some
slight adaptations to our notation. With discrete quality, the optimal price
menu
p(q)
will be constant between integer values of
q
. Thus, we take
prices to be an increasing vector
p^{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{Q}
, where
element
p_{i}^{x}=p^{x}(i)
,
i\in\{1,\dots,Q\}
. Rather than working
with
p
, it is actually easier to work with the
incremental price

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}^{x}=p_{i}^{x}-p_{i-1}^{x}
. This is because we know
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}^{x}=\min\{v\mid{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 286$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 286$}}}^{x}(v)\geq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}\}
. Note that
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}^{x}
is the lowest consumer type who purchases quality
i
, and thus is
also increasing in
i
.

This model is equivalent to one of a multi-good monopolist, where the seller
sells
Q
goods which are identical to the consumer but have increasing
marginal cost of production
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}
. Then,
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}
corresponds to the
price charged for each good, which is a “quality increment” in our
original model. We will sometimes refer to
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}
as the price of
increment or unit
i
.

Consider the analysis of Section
7
in this setting. Given
any increasing sequence
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}
,
w_{k}(h)
is constant except at the
jump points of
Q(v_{k}-h)
. Hence, to achieve the Pareto efficient
frontier, it is without loss to restrict to distributions such that
v_{k}-h_{k}^{x}\in\{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}\}
; these are the only hazard rates
necessary to obtain the concavification of
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}_{k,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}
, no matter
what
\textstyle\mathchar 277
is.

Lemma B.3 (Sufficient Distributions)

When
c
is discrete, the Pareto frontier can be achieved
with segmentations supported only on distributions
x
such that
x
is
regular and for every
k
,
v_{k}-h_{k}^{x}\in\{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}\}
.

These distributions have a very particular structure: for every
k
,
h_{k}^{x}=v_{k}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,Q\}
, where
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}
increases with
k
. Equivalently, there is some partition of
V
into
i
different (possibly empty) sections
R_{i}=[r_{i},r_{i+1})
, such that
for any
v_{k}\in R_{i}
,

v_{k}-h_{k}^{x}={{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}\implies D^{x}_{k+1}=\frac{v_{k}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}}{v_{k+1}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}}\cdot D^{x}_{k}.

This distribution can be written explicitly as

D^{x}_{k}=\frac{A_{i}}{v_{k}-c_{i}}\quad\forall v_{k}\in(r_{i},r_{i+1}]

where
A_{1}\triangleq r_{1}-c_{1}
and

A_{i+1}\triangleq A_{i}\frac{r_{i+1}-c_{i+1}}{r_{i+1}-c_{i}}.

Within the sections
R_{i}
, this distribution is what is known as the
generalized Pareto distribution
, defined by:

F(x)=1-\left(1+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}\frac{x-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}\right)^{-1/{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}

where the parameters
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}
are called the location, scale, and
shape, respectively.

Let
D_{r}^{Q}
denote the set of piecewise generalized Pareto distributions
with support
Q
, where
r
is the vector of cutoffs
r_{i}\geq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}

which define the regions
R_{i}
. In Figure
B.1
we
illustrate a few examples of the piecewise Pareto demand functions on the
continuous value set
V=[0,1]
. By construction, within each segment, the
marginal revenue of selling an additional unit of quality to type
v_{k}
is

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i}
when
v_{k}\in R_{i}
, meaning that
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}^{x}=r_{i}
.

Figure B.1
:
Examples of piecewise Pareto demands.

Intuitively, these distributions place the greatest amount of mass possible
to the right of
r_{i}
without violating the requirement that
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}^{x}_{i}=r_{i}
. This intuition is formalized below.

Proposition B.2 (Stochastic Dominance)

Take any
x\in\Delta S
and
r
such that
r_{i}\geq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}^{x}
for all
i
. Then:

D^{x}\left(v_{k}\right)\leq D_{r}^{Q}\left(v_{k}\right),\ \forall v_{k}\in Q.

That is,
x
is first-order stochastically dominated by
D_{r}^{Q}
.

Proof.

For our discussion of discrete qualities, we need some additional notation.
Let

\Pi_{i}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})\triangleq D^{x}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{i})

be the profit attributable to selling the
i
th quality increment at the
incremental price
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}
. We also denote the total profit by

\Pi(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}})\triangleq\sum_{i}\Pi_{i}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}).

We claim that given any two vectors
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}},\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}
with
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}\leq\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}
for all
i
,
D_{\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}}
first-order stochastically
dominates any
x\in X_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}
. That is, for all
v_{k}\in V
,
D^{x}_{k}\leq D_{\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}}(v_{k}).
Suppose, by contradiction, there
exists
\hat{v}
such that
D^{x}(\hat{v})>D_{\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}}(\hat{v})
, and
without loss assume
\hat{v}
is the smallest such value. Let
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}
be
such that
\hat{v}\in({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i+1}]
. Note that
\hat{v}>\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{1}\geq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{1}
, so such
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}
exists. We then have that:

\Pi_{i}(x,\hat{v})>\Pi_{i}(D_{\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}},\hat{v})\geq\Pi_{i}(D_{\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})\geq\Pi_{i}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}).

The first inequality follows from
D^{x}(\hat{v})>D_{\hat{p}}(\hat{v})
. The
second inequality follows from Lemma
B.4
and using that
\hat{v}\in[{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i+1}]
so
\hat{v}<\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i+1}
.
The third inequality follows from the fact that
D^{x}(v)\leq D_{\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}}(v)
for
v<\hat{v}
. But, this implies that
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}
is not an optimal
price for increment
i
, a contradiction. Hence, for any
x\in X_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}
,
D_{\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}}
first-order stochastically dominates
D^{x}
. Finally, it is
immediate that
D_{r}
first-order stochastic dominates any
D_{r}^{Q}

where
Q\subset V
.

This property of the piecewise generalized Pareto also means that it solves
the multi-unit version of the consumer maximization problem studied in
Condorelli and
Szentes 2020
, a feature we discuss in Section
B.3
.

Next, we define the extreme point problem under consideration. Given
\textstyle\mathchar 282
,
we can define the (possibly empty) set of markets where
\textstyle\mathchar 282
is optimal:

X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}=\big\{x\mid{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}^{x}={{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}\big\}=\left\{x\mid p^{x}_{i}=\sum_{j\leq i}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j}\right\}.

As mentioned previously, the set
X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
is a compact, convex subset of
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}
, and equal to the convex hull of its extreme points.

Lemma
B.3
states that the set of all such
D_{r}^{Q}
, where we
vary over all
Q\subseteq V
and increasing sequences
r\in Q^{Q}
, is
sufficient to achieve all Pareto efficient segmentations. This is because
they are the only distributions needed to support
\overline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 289$}}}}_{k,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}
for any
\textstyle\mathchar 277
. It turns out that these distributions are
also exactly the extreme points of
X_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}
.

Proposition B.3 (Extreme points of
X_{p}
)

With discrete qualities,
x
is an extreme point of
X_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}
if and only if
D^{x}=D_{r}^{Q}
for some
Q\subseteq V
and
r_{i}\leq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}\leq r_{i+1}
for all
i\in\{1,2,\dots,Q\}
.

Proof.

We begin with two lemmas. The first tells us how profits co-move with prices
and costs. The second establishes that
X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
is nonempty if and only if
D_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
exists.

Lemma B.4 (Co-monotonicity of Prices and Costs)

For any
i,j
such that
c_{i}>c_{j}
and
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i},\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}
such that
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}>\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}
(or
c_{i}<c_{j}
and
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}<\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}
):

\Pi_{i}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})\leq\Pi_{i}(x,\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i})\implies\Pi_{j}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})<\Pi_{j}(x,\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}).

Proof.

We have:

\Pi_{i}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})\leq\Pi_{i}(x,\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i})\implies\frac{D^{x}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})}{D^{x}(\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i})}\leq\frac{\hat{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}-c_{i}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}-c_{i}}<\frac{\hat{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}-c_{j}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}-c_{j}}\implies\Pi_{j}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})<\Pi_{j}(x,\hat{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}),

as desired. The case of
c_{i}<c_{j}
can be proven similarly by inverting the
ratios.

Lemma B.5 (Existence of
X_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}
)

X_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}
is non-empty if and only if for all
i\in\{1,..,Q\}
: (i)
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}\leq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i+1}
and (ii)
c_{i}<{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}
.

Proof.

For prices satisfying these conditions,
D_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}\in X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
and so
X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}

is non-empty. For necessity,
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}<c_{i}
is obviously never optimal, and
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}>{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i+1}
cannot be optimal by Lemma
B.4
.

We are now ready to prove the proposition.

(
\Rightarrow
). First, we show that if
x\in X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
is an extreme point,
then every
v_{k}\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}(x)
is an optimal price for some unit
i
.
To see this, suppose there is a
v_{k}
such that
x_{k}>0
, but, for every
i
,

v_{k}\notin\argmax_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}}\Pi_{i}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}).

Consider the following market segmentation with uniform distribution and
binary support in markets
\{x_{-},x_{+}\}
defined as follows:

D_{x_{-}}(v)=\begin{cases}D(v)&\text{if }v\neq v_{k};\\
D(v_{k})-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}&\text{if }v=v_{k},\end{cases}\quad D_{x_{+}}(v)=\begin{cases}D(v)&\text{if }v\neq v_{k};\\
D(v_{k})+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 271$}}}&\text{if }v=v_{k}.\end{cases}

The segmentations clearly conform to the aggregate market. Furthermore, if
\textstyle\mathchar 271
is small enough, then we continue to have that for all
i
,

v_{k}\notin\argmax_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}}\Pi_{i}(x_{-},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})\text{ and }v_{k}\notin\argmax_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}}\Pi_{i}(x_{+},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}).

Hence,
x_{+},\ x_{-}\in X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}},
contradicting that
x
is an extreme point.
Thus, every extreme point satisfies:

D^{x}_{k}=\frac{\Pi_{i}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})}{v_{k}-c_{i}},

for some
i
. To finish the proof, we need to show that the constants
\Pi_{i}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})
are consistent with the piecewise Pareto. This is
equivalent to showing that
\overline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}^{x}=\underline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i+1}^{x}

whenever
c_{i}<c_{i+1}
, where

\overline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}^{x}=\max\left\{\argmax_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}}\Pi_{i}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})\right\}

is the largest optimal price for increment
i
, and similarly for
\underline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}^{x}
. Suppose
\overline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}^{x}<\underline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i+1}^{x}
, and let
i
be the smallest index where this fails. Consider the
following segmentation, again with uniform distribution over binary support:

D_{x_{-}}(v)=\begin{cases}D^{x}(v)&\text{if }v<\underline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i+1}^{x};\\
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}D^{x}(v)&\text{if }v\geq\underline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i+1}^{x},\end{cases}\quad D_{x_{+}}(v)=\begin{cases}D^{x}(v)&\text{if }v<\underline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i+1}^{x};\\
(2-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}})D^{x}(v)&\text{if }v\geq\underline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i+1}^{x},\end{cases}

where
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}<1
. We claim that if
\textstyle\mathchar 276
is sufficiently close to 1,
x_{-},x_{+}\in X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
.

Take
x_{-}
(a similar argument works for
x_{+}
). If any prices changed, it
is of some unit in
\{i+1,\dots,n\}
. But, for any unit
j\geq i+1
and
v_{k}<\underline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i+1}^{x}
,

\Pi_{j}(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j})\geq\Pi_{j}(x,\underline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i+1}^{x})>\Pi_{j}(x,\overline{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}_{i}^{x})\geq\Pi_{j}(x,v_{k}),

where the first inequality comes from optimality of price
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j}
, and the
next two follow from Lemma
B.4
. Thus, for
\textstyle\mathchar 276

sufficiently close to 1, we have
x_{-},x_{+}\in X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
, a contradiction.

(
\Leftarrow
). By Proposition
B.2
,
D_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
first-order
stochastically dominates every element of
X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
. Hence, it cannot be
written as the convex combination of two elements in
X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
. Additionally,
by Lemma
B.5
,
D_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
exists if and only if
X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
is
non-empty, and hence has extreme points.

We can visualize this result using the simplex. Figure
B.2

represents the simplex
\Delta V
when there are three values and two
products. The different regions
X_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}
are identified by a vector that
identifies the optimal (incremental) prices in each region.

Figure B.2
:
Division of
\Delta V
into
X_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}}
with
V=\{1,2,3\}

and
c=(0,\frac{1}{3})
.

The extreme points of
X_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
are the vertices of each price region. In
Figure
B.2
there are three interior extremal markets,
illustrated with white, red and blue dots. The white dot is a market in
which the seller is indifferent between the three prices
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}\in\{1,2,3\}

for quality 1 and the optimal price for the second unit of quality is
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}=3.
The blue dot is a market in which the seller is indifferent between
the three prices
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}\in\{1,2,3\}
for the second unit of quality and the
optimal price for the quality 1 is
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}=1
. The red dot is a market such
that the seller is indifferent between the prices
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}\in\{1,2\}
for
quality 1 and is indifferent between the prices
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}\in\{2,3\}
for the
second unit. There are other extremal markets in which the support of the
distribution is smaller. The extremal markets marked by gray dots illustrate
points in which all buyers have value 3, and another one in which the buyer
never has a value of 2, but the seller is indifferent between the prices
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}\in\{2,3\}
for the second unit.

Looking at Figure
B.2
, it is clear that the intersections of
the lines are the only points which are not the convex combination of any
two other points in the price region. The proof makes this observation
rigorous by explicitly checking that every point apart from these
intersections can be written as the convex combination of two other points
within the same price region. For the points where some value
v_{k}\in\mathop{\mathrm{s}upp}(x)
is not an optimal price for some unit, we can easily perturb

x
in opposite directions without changing the optimal price vector. This
leaves us with just those markets
x
which are along the boundary. For
these markets, we verify that there always exists two “neighboring” extremal markets such that
x
lies in the
convex combination of the two.

B.3
Unconstrained Consumer Maximization

The
unconstrained consumer maximization
problem asks which
distribution
x
leads to the highest consumer surplus
U(x)
. Formally, it
solves

\max_{x\in\Delta V}\Big[U(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}^{x})\Big]\text{ such that }{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}^{x}\in\argmax_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}\in V^{Q}}\big[\Pi(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}})\big].

(B.2)

We call this problem
unconstrained
because it is essentially the same
as (
6
) without the majorization constraint.

It is easy to see that increasing the distribution of values will increase
profit. In fact, 0 and
v_{K}
are the profit when the distribution of
values is degenerate at 0 or
v_{K}
, respectively, and everything in
between is achievable with an intermediate distribution. When we examine how
the distribution of values impacts consumer surplus, however, the effect is
more subtle. In particular, if the distribution is degenerate, the seller
extracts the full surplus. Hence, the problem is not trivial.

The solution to (
B.2
) with a continuum of values when
Q=1

and
c=0
is given by
Condorelli and
Szentes 2020
. The optimal distribution is a Pareto
distribution with shape 1 and scale parameter
\frac{1}{e}
, truncated on
the interval
[\frac{1}{e},1]
. With
Q=1
, the assumption that
c=0
is
without loss, but for our purposes, it is convenient to “undo” this
normalization.

Proposition B.4 (Consumer-Optimal Distribution—Single Good)

The solution to (
B.2
) when
Q=1
is

D^{x}(v)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }v<{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}};\\
\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}-c}{v-c}&\text{if }v\geq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}},\end{cases}

(B.3)

for some
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}>c
.

The distribution is constructed as to keep profit constant for every price
in
[{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}},v_{K}]
, and (given this indifference) the seller sells the
product at the lowest optimal price
\textstyle\mathchar 282
. The proof of this result then
shows that, given this complete indifference, any other distribution which
induces price
\textstyle\mathchar 282
is first order stochastically dominated by (
B.3
), and hence gives lower consumer surplus.

We know that the piecewise generalized Pareto distributions also maintain
constant profits across the relevant regions, and by Proposition
B.2
they also first order stochastically dominate any other
distribution with the same or lower prices. Thus, we can apply the same
proof for the multi-unit case on these piecewise Pareto distributions.

Proposition B.5 (Consumer-Optimal Distribution)

If
(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}})
is a solution to the consumer surplus
maximization problem (
B.2
), then the optimal demand function is
given by
D_{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
for some
\textstyle\mathchar 282
.

For a given cost vector
c
, the solution
(x,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}})
includes a particular
price vector
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}\in V^{Q}
that maximizes the consumer surplus in the
market
x
. Figure
B.3
plots the optimal demand function for
two products,
Q=2
, and the cost vector
c=(0,0.5)
. The resulting solution
is compared with the corresponding single product solutions for
c=0
and
c=0.5
. In general, the optimal values of
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}
are non-elementary
expressions of
c
.

Figure B.3
:
Consumer-Optimal demands.

We can provide a sharper characterization of the optimal solution than
offered by Proposition
B.2
in the case of binary products,
Q=2
. When there are only 2 product for sale, it is without loss of
generality to normalize
c_{1}=0
, so that the model is parametrized only by

c_{2}
, which we refer to
c
in the remainder of this Subsection. It is
useful to consider the limit as the set of values becomes more refined. For
this purpose, we define:

\Delta\triangleq\max_{k}\{v_{k+1}-v_{k}\}

as we let the number of values become arbitrarily large,
K\rightarrow\infty
.

Proposition B.6 (Prices with Two Goods)

When
Q=2
, there exists
\bar{c}
such that in the
consumer-optimal distribution of values, the seller bundles the products (
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{1}={{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}
) if and only if
c\leq\bar{c}
. Furthermore,

\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}\overline{c}\rightarrow\frac{v_{K}}{1+e}.

Proof.

We first compute
U(x)
for extremal markets
x=D_{p}
in the limit where
V=[0,1]
. The consumer surplus from selling unit
i
to consumers with
valuations
v\in[{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j+1})
is:

A_{j}\int_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j}}^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j+1}}\frac{v-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}}{(v-c_{j})^{2}}\ dv=A_{j}\left(\log\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j+1}-c_{j}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j}-c_{j}}-\frac{({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}-c_{j})({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j+1}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j})}{({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j+1}-c_{j})({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j}-c_{j})}\right).

The total consumer surplus is the above expression summed over all
i
and
j\geq i
, plus the consumer surplus of the mass point at the end,
\frac{A_{Q}}{1-c_{Q}}\sum_{i}(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})
. Summing everything together, we get:

U(x,p)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[iA_{i}\log\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i+1}-c_{i}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}-c_{i}}+\frac{A_{Q}}{1-c_{Q}}(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})-\sum_{j\geq i}A_{j}\frac{({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}-c_{j})({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j+1}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j})}{({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j+1}-c_{j})({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{j}-c_{j})}\right].

We claim that the non-logarithmic terms cancel out. To see this, fix an
i
,
then take the difference between the middle term and the
j=Q
summand of
the last term:

\frac{A_{Q}}{1-c_{Q}}\left[1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}-\frac{({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}-c_{Q})(1-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{Q})}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{Q}-c_{Q}}\right]=\frac{A_{Q}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{Q}-c_{Q}}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{Q}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i})=\frac{A_{n-1}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{Q}-c_{n-1}}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{Q}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}).

Repeat this recursively for remaining
j
, and at the last step the sum
becomes 0, giving us

U(x,p)=\sum_{i=1}^{Q}\left[iA_{i}\log\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i+1}-c_{i}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{i}-c_{i}}.\right]

(B.4)

(
B.4
) reduces finding the optimal
p
to a constrained
optimization problem, subject to
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{1}\leq{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}
. The KKT conditions
are:

\displaystyle{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}=\log\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{1}}+2\left(1-\frac{c}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}}\right)\log\frac{1-c}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}-c}-1=\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{1}}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}}-2\left(\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{1}c}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}^{2}}\right)\log\frac{1-c}{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}-c}

(FOC)

\displaystyle{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}-{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{1})=0.

(CS)

We can check when
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{1}={{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}
is a solution. Plugging in
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{1}={{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}_{2}={{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}
yields
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 282$}}}=\frac{1}{e}(1-c)+c
. Dual feasibility then
requires that:

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}=\frac{2(1-c)}{1+(e-1)c}-1\geq 0\iff c\leq\frac{1}{1+e}

as desired.

This result can be compared with
Haghpanah and
Hartline 2021
, which discusses conditions
under which bundling is optimal for the seller. Figure
B.4

plots the prices the seller charges against the consumer-optimal
distribution as a function of
c
, in the limit where
V=[0,1]
. We can see
that when the units have a similar cost, it is optimal to force the seller
to bundle the units together. But, as
c
increases above
\overline{c}=\frac{1}{1+e}
, the price of unit 1 falls while the price for unit 2 rises.

Figure B.4
:
Prices under consumer-optimal demand when
Q=2
.
